# Acetabular Fractures

*Trauma · 1 / 67*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **\~4 / 100,000 / yr**

**Bimodal** — **young + high energy** (MVC) · **elderly + low energy** (fall from standing)

**Elderly volume** — \~4,000/yr in USA → may become the commonest age group

| Age group | Commonest patterns |
|---|---|
| **Young** | **Posterior wall** · transverse "family" (transverse, T-type, transverse + PW) · associated both column |
| **Elderly** | **Anterior wall** · **anterior column (quadrilateral plate)** · **anterior column posterior hemitransverse (ACPHT)** · associated both column |

**Elderly low-energy fall onto greater trochanter** — **anterior column + "medial wall"** (quadrilateral plate) ± intrapelvic head dislocation\
✗ Wrong options: PC + PW · transverse · T-type · T + PW → young, high energy

### Associated injuries

| Associated injury | Rate |
|---|---|
| **Lower extremity injury** | **36%** (commonest) |
| Head injury | 19% |
| Chest injury | 18% |
| **Nerve palsy** | **13%** (3–33% in OB) |
| Abdominal | 8% |
| Genitourinary | 6% |
| Spine | 4% |

**Nerve palsy pattern** — **sciatic, peroneal division** → **foot drop**; highest with **transverse + posterior wall**

**Nerve-injury share by pattern (OB)** — PW 9.5–23% · ABC 15.6% · PC + PW 12.5–23% · isolated PC rarely

**Root most often involved** — **L5** (→ tibialis anterior, EHL, EDB)\
✗ Wrong options: L4, S1, S2, S3

**Isolated peroneal-division injury** — commoner than complete sciatic injury

**Posterior hip dislocation + deceleration** — think **traumatic thoracic aortic rupture**; CXR may be normal → chest pain + hypotension + new leg weakness (cord ischaemia) → **CT angiography of chest**\
✗ Wrong options: CT head · CT pelvis · CT L-spine · leg angiogram

### Anatomy – column theory

**Columns** — **inverted Y**, linked to sacrum through the **sciatic buttress**

| | Anterior column | Posterior column |
|---|---|---|
| Parts | Anterior ilium (gluteus medius tubercle), **anterior wall + dome**, iliopectineal eminence, lateral superior pubic ramus | **Quadrilateral surface**, **posterior wall + dome**, **ischial tuberosity**, greater/lesser sciatic notches |
| Radiographic line | **Iliopectineal line** | **Ilioischial line** |
| Best Judet | **Obturator oblique** | **Iliac oblique** |

**TO anatomy (TO)** — anterior column = anterior ½ iliac crest, iliac spines, anterior ½ acetabulum, pubis · posterior column = ischium, ischial spine, posterior ½ acetabulum, dense sciatic-notch bone

**Acetabular orientation** — lateral inclination **40–48°** · anteversion **18–21°**

### Corona mortis & vascular anatomy

**Corona mortis ("crown of death")** — anastomosis **external iliac (inferior epigastric) ↔ obturator** (internal iliac system) behind **superior pubic ramus**

**Prevalence (Tornetta)** — **84%** have an anastomosis · **arterial 34%**, **venous 70%**, both 20%

**Distance from symphysis** — **mean 6.2 cm (range 3–9 cm)** → "brisk bleeding \~5 cm from midline along superior ramus"

**At risk** — **ilioinguinal** (laceration **10–15%**) + **modified Stoppa** (must be **exposed + ligated**); retracts into pelvis → hard to control

**✗ Vessel traps** — superior gluteal + pudendal · internal iliac + MFCA · external iliac + deep femoral · femoral branch

![Corona mortis: inferior epigastric–obturator anastomosis](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1034-this-illustration-demonstrates-the-anastomosis-between-the-i.jpg)

## 2 Mechanism

**Pattern determined by** — **force vector** + **femoral head position** at impact + **bone quality** (age)

**Dashboard / posteriorly directed force** — posterior wall ± posterior dislocation

**Crescent fracture (posterior iliac wing on CT)** — **lateral compression** (laterally directed force on anterior iliac wing)\
✗ Wrong options: APC · VS · combined

## 3 Presentation

**Foot drop / ↓ dorsiflexion after acetabular fracture** — **peroneal division of sciatic** → most likely pattern **transverse + posterior wall**

**Hip flexed, adducted, IR** — posterior dislocation ± posterior wall

**Os acetabuli marginalis superior** — accessory ossification centre at superior acetabular rim in adolescents; **mimics fracture / AVN**; usually **fuses by age 20**\
✗ Wrong options: fovea capitis · myositis ossificans · AVN · fracture

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Shows / use |
|---|---|
| **AP pelvis** | Screening; 6 landmarks: **iliopectineal line (AC)**, **ilioischial line (PC)**, anterior wall, posterior wall, **teardrop**, **weight-bearing roof** (+ Shenton's line) |
| **Obturator oblique (Judet)** | Obturator foramen in profile → **anterior column + posterior wall** (**"OOPWAC"**, TO) |
| **Iliac oblique (Judet)** | Iliac wing in profile, obturator foramen perpendicular → **posterior column + anterior wall** |
| Inlet / outlet | Only if pelvic ring involvement suspected |
| **CT** | **Gold standard**: pattern orientation, fragment size + position, **marginal impaction**, **loose bodies** (post-reduction), gap / step-off, surgical planning; 3D recon for planning |
| CT not for | **Pre-existing degenerative change** (EXCEPT answer) |
| **Post-op CT** | **Most accurate for PW reduction** → **best correlation with outcome** |
| **EUA (dynamic fluoroscopy)** | **Best test of posterior wall stability** |
| Duplex US | Delayed presentation → rule out **DVT** |

**Judet = 45° obliques** — ipsilateral **iliac oblique = contralateral obturator oblique** (same image)

**"Best view of LEFT posterior column?"** — **left iliac oblique** (= right obturator oblique)\
✗ Wrong options: left obturator oblique · right iliac oblique

**Judet traps** — PW on **obturator oblique** · OO does **not** show SI joint well\
✗ Wrong options: iliac oblique · inlet · outlet · AP

**Interobserver reliability (Beaulé)** — Letournel substantial (κ > 0.70) **with or without CT** → CT not essential for classification, but adds **impaction + loose bodies**

![Obturator oblique Judet view: anterior column + posterior wall](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1034-obturator-oblique-labeled.jpg)

| Sign / measure | Meaning | View |
|---|---|---|
| **Spur sign** | **Intact ilium** (most caudal part of intact ilium / posteroinferior ilium) exposed as acetabulum medialises → **pathognomonic for associated both column** | **Obturator oblique** (also AP) |
| **Gull (seagull) sign** | **Superomedial dome impaction** → **poor prognosis** (Orthobullets) | **Iliac oblique** (Orthobullets) |
| **Gull sign – TO wording (TO)** | **Anteromedial dome impaction**; "pathognomonic for **posterior column** fractures" → needs surgery | **Obturator oblique** (TO) |
| **Roof arc angle (Matta)** | Vertical line through head centre vs line to fracture; **stable if > 45° on AP + both Judets** (fracture exits outside weight-bearing dome) | AP + Judets |
| **CT subchondral arc (Olson & Matta)** | **Superior 10 mm** of acetabulum intact = equivalent to **45° roof arc** (TO) | CT |
| **Articular incongruity** | **> 2 mm** = unstable | CT |
| **Medial clear space widening on EUA** | **Posterior wall instability** | Obturator oblique under stress |

**Gull sign view** — **iliac oblique** (Orthobullets) · **obturator oblique** (TO) — read the options

**Roof arc NOT applicable** — **posterior wall** + **associated both column** (no intact acetabulum to measure)

**Spur sign absent** — **nondisplaced** ABC → use CT to confirm acetabulum–axial skeleton continuity

![Spur sign on obturator oblique (associated both column)](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1034-spur-sign.jpg)

### Letournel & Judet classification

**Structure** — **5 elementary + 5 associated** = 10 types

![Letournel elementary patterns](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1034-elementary-types.jpg)

| Elementary | Lines / views | Key fact |
|---|---|---|
| **Posterior wall** | PW outline broken on **OO** | **Commonest overall**; check **superior gluteal NV bundle**; only \~30% single large fragment |
| **Posterior column** | **Ilioischial line only** broken on AP; IO shows break, **anterior wall intact**; OO iliopectineal intact | Superior gluteal NV bundle; isolated PC rarely injures nerve |
| **Anterior wall** | — | **Very rare**; **geriatric** |
| **Anterior column** | **Iliopectineal line broken on OO** with **undisturbed posterior acetabulum** | 3–5% of fractures; may extend to quadrilateral plate without exiting PC |
| **Transverse** | **Iliopectineal + ilioischial** both broken; single line crossing acetabulum | **Only elementary to involve both columns**; axial CT: **vertical (sagittal) A→P line**; articular surface still attached to intact ilium |

![Letournel associated patterns](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1034-associated-types.jpg)

| Associated | Key fact |
|---|---|
| **Associated both column (ABC)** | **All articular segments detached from intact ilium** (which stays on sacrum via SI joint) → **no articular surface attached to axial skeleton** · **spur sign** · may show **secondary congruence** |
| **Transverse + posterior wall** | **Commonest associated** · **highest nerve injury** · high DJD risk |
| **T-type** | Transverse + vertical split into **obturator ring** · high DJD risk |
| **ACPHT** | **Commonest in elderly** |
| **PC + PW** | **Only associated type NOT involving both columns** |

**Column vs transverse on axial CT** — **transverse = vertical (sagittal) line** · **column = horizontal (coronal) line**

**"Only one column involved?"** — **PC + PW**\
✗ Wrong options: ABC · transverse · T + PW

**Line-pattern traps** — both lines + **iliac wing + obturator ring** = ABC or ACPHT · both lines + **obturator ring** = T-type · iliopectineal only = AC · ilioischial only = PC

**Distinguishing ABC from ACPHT / T / T + PW** — those keep **part of articular surface on intact ilium**

### Posterior wall size & stability

| PW involvement | Stability | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **< 20%** | Generally stable | Protected WB — but **EUA if fracture exits superiorly (cranial exit near dome)** |
| **20–40%** | **Indeterminate** | **EUA** |
| **> 40–50%** | **Unstable** | **ORIF** |

**Fragment-size methods (less accurate than EUA)** — **Keith** (depth vs contralateral **at fovea**) · **Moed** (depth vs contralateral **at greatest involvement**) · **Calkins** (posterior **arc length** at greatest involvement)

**EUA technique** — hip **flexion + adduction + axial load**, **obturator oblique** view → **↑ medial clear space** = unstable

**History of dislocation** — **not** predictive of instability (Grimshaw & Moed: stable on stress exam → nonop fine)

**Not instability predictors** — fragment size alone, dislocation history, PW comminution, roof arc, MRI labral tear, iliac oblique

## 5 Management

```
Acetabular fracture
├─ Associated hip dislocation → urgent closed reduction (< 12 h) → CT
│      └─ incarcerated fragment → traction pin → ORIF ASAP
├─ Stable / minimally displaced / high-risk patient → protected WB 6–8 wk
└─ Displaced roof > 2 mm, unstable, impaction, loose body → ORIF < 3 wk
       └─ elderly + impaction / comminution / head injury → ORIF + acute THA
```

```
Posterior wall fracture (post-reduction CT)
├─ < 20%, exits low → protected WB
├─ < 20% exiting near dome, or 20–40% → EUA (obturator oblique)
│      └─ unstable → ORIF
└─ > 40–50% or intra-articular fragment → ORIF
```

### Nonoperative criteria

| Patient factors | Fracture factors |
|---|---|
| High operative risk (elderly, **DVT**) | **Minimally displaced < 2 mm** |
| **Morbid obesity** | **< 20% posterior wall** (EUA best test) |
| **Open contaminated wound** | **Head congruent with roof out of traction** |
| **Late presentation > 3 wk** | **ABC with secondary congruence** |
| | **Roof arcs > 45°** on AP + Judets |

**Nonop regimen** — protected WB **6–8 wk**; **skeletal traction NOT needed** if stable outside dome; **DVT prophylaxis**; serial X-rays

**Elderly ABC with secondary congruence** — **nonop**\
✗ Wrong options: ORIF + THA

### Operative criteria

| Patient factors | Fracture factors |
|---|---|
| **< 3 wk from injury** | **Roof displacement > 2 mm** |
| Physiologically stable | **Unstable pattern (PW > 40–50%)** |
| Adequate soft tissue | **Marginal impaction** |
| No local infection | **Intra-articular loose bodies** |
| **Pregnancy is NOT a contraindication** | **Irreducible fracture-dislocation** |

**Absolute surgical indication after dislocation** — **osseous loose body in weight-bearing joint**\
✗ Wrong options: sciatic palsy · labral tear · PW fracture per se · chondral defect

### Fracture-dislocation – reduction, traction & ORIF

**Posterior dislocation + small PW fracture** — **reduce hip now, test stability**\
✗ Wrong options: CT first · MRI · open reduction · transfer before reduction

**After reduction** — **CT mandatory** even if no fracture on X-ray → incarcerated osteochondral fragments · sciatic palsy \~10%\
✗ Wrong options: exploration of nerve · EMG · traction

**Reduction timing** — **< 12 h** (Orthobullets; > 12 h delay = poor result, OB) · **< 6 h** to minimise AVN (one OB explanation) — read the options

**Incarcerated fragment after reduction** — **distal femoral traction pin + ORIF ASAP** (offloads cartilage)\
✗ Wrong options: knee immobiliser + delayed ORIF · nonop

**Incarcerated fragment + shock / unresuscitated + knee laxity** — **distal femoral** pin\
✗ Wrong options: proximal tibial pin with knee ligament injury · emergent ORIF · binder · EUA with intra-articular fragment

**Marginal impaction on CT** — must be **elevated + bone grafted + buttress plated**; otherwise incongruent joint → poor result

**Loose fragments** — remove, but **incorporate** large articular fragments into fixation

**Pregnancy (12 wk, normal fetal HR, unstable hip)** — **acute ORIF**\
✗ Wrong options: wait till 15 wk · traction 6–8 wk

**Fetal mortality ↑ with** — **injury severity, mechanism of injury, maternal haemorrhage**\
✗ Wrong options: trimester · approach · classification · fetal position

### Timing of surgery

**Worse outcome if fixed** — **> 3 wk** from injury\
✗ Wrong options: 48 h · 5 d · 1 wk · 2 wk

**Delay > 11 days** — ↓ rate of anatomic reduction (OB)

**↑ Time to surgery** — **↓ chance of anatomic reduction** · infection trend ↑ · **no** effect on HO or neuro injury

**Early fixation → ↓** LOS, **↓ organ dysfunction**, ↑ function, ↑ discharge home, ↑ reduction quality (EXCEPT trap: "greater organ dysfunction")

### Surgical approach selection

| Approach | Indications | Risks |
|---|---|---|
| **Kocher-Langenbeck** (posterior) | **PW, PC, PC + PW, most transverse + T** | **Sciatic nerve (2–10%)**, **MFCA** (head blood supply), **↑ HO vs anterior** |
| **Ilioinguinal** (anterior) | **AW, AC, ABC (standard), ACPHT** | **Femoral nerve**, **LFCN**, femoral vessel thrombosis, **corona mortis 10–15%** · **lowest HO** |
| **Modified Stoppa (anterior intrapelvic, AIP)** | **Quadrilateral plate** buttress (medial wall comminution, ABC); ± lateral window ≈ ilioinguinal access | Corona mortis **must be ligated** |
| **Extended iliofemoral** | **Only single approach to see both columns directly**; T-type / transverse with **posterior comminution**, **transtectal T**, ABC with **posterior column comminution**, **SI joint involvement**, **> 3 wk** delay | **Massive HO (highest)**, **gluteal muscle necrosis** |
| **Iliofemoral** | **High anterior column** only | Not ABC |
| **Modified Gibson** | Gluteus max–TFL interval ± **trochanteric flip**; PW/PC, selected T + transverse | — |
| **Trochanteric flip osteotomy + surgical dislocation** | **PW + femoral head fracture (Pipkin IV)** | — |

**Stöckle (extended iliofemoral)** — 80% anatomic (< 1 mm) · loss of reduction 8% · **HO grade 3 13%** · AVN 4%

**ABC with spur sign, intact SI + PW, acute** — **ilioinguinal**\
✗ Wrong options: extended iliofemoral · iliofemoral · KL · Gibson

**74-yo, anterior column** — **ilioinguinal**\
✗ Wrong options: Watson-Jones = hip · Hardinge = lateral THA

**Transverse + PW** — **KL** (✗ Wrong options: trochanteric flip · extended iliofemoral) · **LC2 crescent** — anterior or posterior (✗ Wrong options: extended)

**Approach does not affect outcome** (Matta)

### Sciatic nerve – positioning & injury

**Least sciatic tension during KL** — **hip extended + knee flexed (90°)**\
✗ Wrong options: hip flexed · knee extended · ilioinguinal (that risks femoral + LFCN)

**Highest intraneural pressure** — **hip flexed 90° + knee extended** (excursion from sciatic notch to leg)

### Percutaneous column screws – fluoroscopic views

| View | Shows |
|---|---|
| **Obturator outlet ("teepee" / teardrop)** | **Joint penetration** · **cranial–caudal** position · **supra-acetabular start point** (AIIS pin) |
| **Iliac inlet (inlet iliac oblique)** | **Anterior–posterior position in pubic ramus** → screw not exiting **posterior ramus** / not intrapelvic |
| **Obturator inlet** | Supra-acetabular screw **between inner + outer iliac tables** |
| **Iliac oblique** | Trajectory **above sciatic notch** · posterior column screw |
| **Inlet** | **SI joint penetration** |
| IO / OO with **hip + knee flexed** | **Posterior column screw** |

**Teardrop on obturator outlet** — column of bone **AIIS → PSIS** (ex-fix pins / screws)\
✗ Wrong options: ASIS · sciatic buttress · roof

**Antegrade AC screw** — start **gluteal pillar** → toward pubic tubercle · retrograde = ramus → iliac wing

### Elderly – acute THA with / without ORIF

**Acute THA indications** — elderly + **osteopenia / comminution**, **pre-existing arthritis**, **marginal impaction (gull sign)**, **PW comminution**, **femoral head fracture / damage**, **superomedial dome impaction**, **dislocation**, **delayed presentation**

**Elderly PW + dislocation + impaction / comminution / head damage** — **acute ORIF + immediate THA** (single stage) → **superior function** vs ORIF + delayed THA

**THA construct** — wall fractures → **buttress plate + multi-hole cup** · column fractures → **cage + cup** · columns must be stable first

**78-yo, posterior column + femoral head fracture** — **PC ORIF + THA same sitting**\
✗ Wrong options: cup screws alone → cup migration, LaFlamme · staged · cup-cage (only for protrusio / discontinuity)

**74-yo neglected insufficiency fracture, 4 mo, head damage** — **hip reconstruction (THA, flanged / custom cup)**\
✗ Wrong options: ORIF · traction

**Fracture-dislocation + articular injury + head impaction + loose bodies, ORIF alone** — **\~50% convert to THA**; THA → **better function, fewer complications**

**Kreder** — **> 50 yr + marginal impaction + PW comminution** → **54%** early THA; residual displacement **> 2 mm** → arthritis

**THA after acetabular fracture** — **> 60 yr: \~30% late conversion** · 10-yr implant survival **75–80%**

**Acute ORIF + THA not for** — young patients · isolated AC · small (10–20%) PW · transverse with good bone · ABC with secondary congruence

### Postoperative weight bearing & hip contact pressures

| Activity | Hip contact force |
|---|---|
| **Rising from chair on affected leg** | **Highest** (> walking); **posterosuperior acetabulum** |
| Full WB gait | High |
| Isometrics, bed pan | Approach walking, not exceed |
| Cane | \~60% of walking |
| **Toe-touch WB + passive abduction** | **Lowest** (< NWB — muscles contract in NWB) |

**Touch WB** — **10–15 kg** (< 20% BW) · partial WB = 20–25 kg (30–50% BW) → after **transtectal transverse PW ORIF**

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Post-traumatic DJD** | **Commonest**; **80% native-hip survival at 20 yr** after ORIF; \~**20% need THA by 20 yr**; risk: **age > 40**, **T + PW and T-type**, **femoral head injury**, marginal impaction, malreduction → **THA or fusion** |
| **Heterotopic ossification** | **Highest extended iliofemoral** · KL intermediate · **lowest ilioinguinal / Stoppa**; \~25% incidence |
| **Osteonecrosis** | **6–7%** overall · **18% posterior patterns** |
| **Nerve injury** | Highest **T + PW**; sciatic (KL 2–10%), femoral / LFCN (ilioinguinal) |
| DVT / PE, infection, bleeding | — |
| Intra-articular hardware | Check obturator outlet |
| Abductor weakness | — |

### Prognostic factors

**Poor outcome** — multi-system trauma · **↑ age** · **poor articular congruency / malreduction** · **marginal impaction** · **femoral head injury** · post-traumatic arthritis · intra-op complications · delay > 3 wk

**Strongest risk for THA** — **articular malreduction** (linear relationship)\
✗ Wrong options: > 50% THA at 20 yr · op time ↓ infection · delay ↓ malreduction · anterior column highest arthritis (PW is)

**Protective vs early THA** — **age < 40**, **simple pattern**, **initial displacement < 20 mm**, anatomic reduction, **no head injury**\
✗ Wrong options: gender · approach

**PW + age > 50 + non-anatomic reduction** — **highest THA conversion**\
✗ Wrong options: AC · PC · AW · ABC

**NOT a negative factor** — **involvement of both columns** (EXCEPT answer) · surgical approach

**Matta results** — excellent 40% · good 36% · fair 8% · poor 16%; clinical result tracks radiographic reduction

**Young, simple transverse, anatomic, no head injury — 20-yr native hip survival** — **> 75%**

**PROs after PW ORIF** — correlate with **hip muscle strength** + gait restoration\
✗ Wrong options: age · arc of motion · immediate WB

### Heterotopic ossification

**Brooker classification** — used to grade HO

![Brooker classification of heterotopic ossification](../../MCQ/attachments/q219166-illa-illustration-a.jpg)

**Prophylaxis** — **indomethacin ×1 wk**, **low-dose radiation**, **debride gluteus minimus** (Orthobullets)

**OB update** — only **radiation 700–800 cGy within 72 h** clearly effective · **indomethacin unproven** in acetabulum + **prolonged use → ↑ nonunion** · gluteus minimus debridement now questioned

**Natural history** — \~85% asymptomatic; \~15% disabling stiffness → **excision** (even early if sciatic compression); **low symptomatic recurrence** (technical \~30%); expect ↑ blood loss

**✗ HO traps** — generous gluteus **medius** debridement (→ Trendelenburg) · "most need excision" · "high symptomatic recurrence" · ISS / fracture pattern as cause · radiation causing HO

**T-type via extended iliofemoral, HO at 3 mo despite radiation** — cause = **surgical approach**

## X vs Y

| | **Obturator oblique** | **Iliac oblique** |
|---|---|---|
| In profile | Obturator foramen | Iliac wing |
| Shows | **Anterior column + posterior wall** | **Posterior column + anterior wall** |
| Signs | **Spur sign**, PW stress EUA | **Gull sign** (Orthobullets) |

| | **Transverse** | **Associated both column** |
|---|---|---|
| Elementary / associated | **Elementary** | Associated |
| Articular surface | Still attached to intact ilium → axial skeleton | **None attached** |
| Lines | Iliopectineal + ilioischial | Both + iliac wing + obturator ring |
| Sign | Vertical line on axial CT | **Spur sign** |

| | **Iliac inlet** | **Obturator outlet** | **Obturator inlet** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Screw check | **AP position in ramus** | **Joint penetration / cranio-caudal** | **Between iliac tables** |

| | **Kocher-Langenbeck** | **Ilioinguinal** | **Extended iliofemoral** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pattern | PW, PC, PC + PW, transverse | AW, AC, ABC, ACPHT | Both columns, > 3 wk, posterior comminution |
| Nerve | **Sciatic** | **Femoral, LFCN** | — |
| HO | Intermediate | **Lowest** | **Highest** |

## How it's asked

**"Elementary fracture involving both columns?"** → **transverse**\
✗ Wrong options: ABC · T-type · T + PW

**"All articular segments detached from intact ilium attached to sacrum?"** → **associated both column**

**"Associated fracture NOT involving both columns?"** → **posterior column + posterior wall**

**"Commonest associated pattern?"** → **transverse + posterior wall**

**"Commonest pattern in elderly?"** → **anterior column posterior hemitransverse** (low-energy: anterior column + medial wall)

**"Spur sign — what and where?"** → **intact ilium; obturator oblique** → **ABC** (TO)

**"Spur sign indicates?"** → **associated both column**\
✗ Wrong options: transtectal transverse · vertical shear · H-type sacral · APC III

**"Obturator oblique shows?"** → **posterior wall + anterior column** (TO)

**"Disruption of which line = posterior column?"** → **ilioischial** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: iliopectineal = anterior column · Shenton · sacral arcuate

**"Anterior column fracture — expected finding?"** → **iliopectineal line broken on obturator oblique, posterior acetabulum intact**

**"Only ilioischial line broken, anterior wall intact on IO?"** → **posterior column**

**"CT indicated for all EXCEPT?"** → **pre-existing degenerative change**

**"Most accurate test of PW stability?"** → **dynamic fluoroscopic EUA, obturator oblique**\
✗ Wrong options: Keith · Moed · Calkins · dislocation history

**"PW < 20% but exits superiorly?"** → **fluoroscopic EUA**\
✗ Wrong options: bedside exam · consent ORIF · angiography · protected WB

**"Post-reduction CT, PW fracture with intra-articular fragment?"** → **traction pin + ORIF**\
✗ Wrong options: EUA · protected WB

**"Hip dislocation reduced, still foot drop?"** → **CT hip**\
✗ Wrong options: explore nerve · EMG

**"Delay most linked to poor result after dislocation?"** → **closed reduction > 12 h**\
✗ Wrong options: > 3 attempts · traction · ORIF > 5 d

**"Delay of fixation associated with poor outcome?"** → **> 3 weeks**

**"Single approach for ABC with segmental posterior column comminution?"** → **extended iliofemoral**

**"Approach for posterior column / PC + PW?"** → **Kocher-Langenbeck**

**"Modified Stoppa for?"** → **quadrilateral plate (ABC with medial wall)**

**"Trochanteric flip osteotomy indicated?"** → **PW + femoral head fracture**

**"Least sciatic tension?"** → **hip extended, knee flexed 90°**

**"Foot drop after acetabular fracture — which pattern?"** → **transverse + posterior wall**

**"Nerve root most affected?"** → **L5**

**"Corona mortis joins external iliac with?"** → **obturator**

**"Retrograde ramus screw — view to avoid posterior exit?"** → **inlet iliac oblique**

**"Obturator oblique-inlet view checks?"** → **screw between inner + outer iliac tables**

**"Obturator oblique-outlet view checks?"** → **cranial–caudal position / joint penetration**

**"Antegrade AC screw — avoid intrapelvic penetration?"** → **iliac oblique inlet**

**"Activity with greatest hip contact force?"** → **rising from chair on affected leg** (posterosuperior acetabulum)

**"Touch WB 10–15 kg most appropriate after?"** → **ORIF transtectal transverse PW**

**"Best predictor of outcome after PW ORIF?"** → **displacement on post-op CT**

**"Improved PROs after PW ORIF?"** → **hip muscle strength**

**"Negative prognostic factors EXCEPT?"** → **involvement of both columns**

**"Strongest risk factor for later THA?"** → **articular malreduction**

**"Protective factor vs early THA conversion?"** → **age < 40**

**"Pattern most linked to THA conversion > 50 yr with malreduction?"** → **posterior wall**

**"Fracture-dislocation with impaction + loose bodies treated with ORIF — THA conversion?"** → **\~50%**

**"Acute ORIF + THA best indication?"** → **elderly, PW + dislocation + marginal impaction / gull sign + femoral head fracture**

**"Elderly posterior column + head fracture?"** → **PC ORIF + THA same procedure**

**"Approach with highest HO (of options)?"** → **Kocher-Langenbeck** (extended iliofemoral highest overall)

**"HO prophylaxis — true?"** → **prolonged indomethacin ↑ nonunion**; only radiation clearly effective

**"Acetabular ORIF in pregnancy — fetal mortality linked to?"** → **mechanism of injury** / injury severity / maternal haemorrhage

**"Posterior dislocation, then chest pain + hypotension + leg weakness?"** → **CT angiography of chest** (aortic injury)

**"Adolescent superior acetabular ossicle?"** → **os acetabuli marginalis superior**

**"Crescent fracture on CT — mechanism?"** → **lateral compression**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Adult Respiratory Distress Syndrome

*Trauma · 2 / 67*

## 1 Basics

### Definition

**ARDS** — acute lung injury after **polytrauma / long-bone fracture** → progressive respiratory distress, **refractory hypoxaemia**, **non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema**

### Causes – acute endothelial damage

**Triggers** — **aspiration**, **infection**, **pancreatitis**, **multiple transfusions**, **lung injury**, **sepsis / shock**, **major trauma**, **large burns**, fat emboli, thromboembolism, MOF

**Cascade** — **non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema** → respiratory distress → **refractory hypoxaemia** → **↓ lung compliance**

### Genes & lab markers

**IL-6 (interleukin 6)** — inflammatory marker **most closely tied to systemic inflammatory response** after orthopaedic injury (**femur fracture**) **and its treatment (IM nailing)**\
✗ Wrong options: IL-1 · IL-10 (no correlation with treatment) · TNF-α · D-dimer (↑ after trauma but don't track treatment)

**Status** — research marker, **not yet clinically applicable**

### Phases

| Phase | Timing | Histology |
|---|---|---|
| **Exudative** | Initial | **Hyaline membranes (fibrin)** |
| **Proliferative** | **\~3 days** | Alveolar exudate resolves or organises |
| **Fibrotic** | **3–4 weeks** | Alveolar ducts + spaces fibrose |

![ARDS histology](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1006-ards.jpg)

## 3 Presentation

**Onset** — acute, **12–48 h** after injury

**Symptoms** — **dyspnoea**, fever, **mottled / cyanotic skin**

**Exam** — **resistant hypoxia**, intercostal retractions, rales / crackles, rhonchi, tachypnoea

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Diagnostic criteria

| PaO₂ / FiO₂ | Grade |
|---|---|
| **< 300 mm Hg** | **Mild** (= acute lung injury, ALI) |
| **< 200 mm Hg** | **Moderate** — **ARDS threshold** |
| **< 100 mm Hg** | **Severe** |

**Other thresholds** — respiratory compliance **< 40 mL/cm H₂O** · PEEP **> 10 cm H₂O** · corrected expired minute volume **> 10 L/min**

**Hallmark** — hypoxaemia **refractory to O₂**

### Key radiology findings

**CXR** — **diffuse bilateral infiltrates**, patchy air-space oedema, **normal-sized heart** (→ CHF less likely)

**Serial CXRs** — early identification in at-risk patients

### Differential

**Mimics** — cardiogenic pulmonary oedema (CHF, MI), bilateral pneumonia, SARS

## 5 Management

**Prevention** — **early stabilisation of long-bone (femur) fractures** · monitor PEEP in at-risk patients · serial CXR

**Treatment** — **PEEP ventilation** + steroids · treat underlying cause

## 6 Complications

**Pneumothorax** — barotrauma from **high-PEEP ventilation**

**Mortality** — **\~50%** even with ICU care

## X vs Y

| | **ARDS** | **Cardiogenic oedema** |
|---|---|---|
| Oedema | **Non-cardiogenic** | Cardiogenic |
| Heart size | **Normal** | Enlarged |
| Response to O₂ | **Refractory** | Responds |

| | **ALI** | **ARDS** |
|---|---|---|
| PaO₂/FiO₂ | **< 300** | **< 200** |

## How it's asked

**"Inflammatory marker most closely tied to systemic response after orthopaedic injury and treatment?"** → **IL-6**\
✗ Wrong options: IL-1 · IL-10 · TNF-α · D-dimer

**"PaO₂/FiO₂ defining ARDS?"** → **< 200 mm Hg** (< 300 = ALI / mild, < 100 = severe)

**"Prevention in polytrauma with femur fracture?"** → **early long-bone stabilisation**

**"Complication of high-PEEP ventilation?"** → **pneumothorax**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Amputations

*Trauma · 3 / 67*

## 1 Basics

### Goals & epidemiology

**Goals** — preserve **functional length** + **useful sensibility** · prevent **symptomatic neuroma** + **adjacent joint contracture** · **early prosthetic fitting** · early return to work

**Prevalence** — **1.7 million** amputees in USA · **80% for vascular insufficiency**

**Commonest cause** — **upper limb = trauma** · **lower limb = vascular disease**

### Wound healing predictors & level selection

| Predictor | Threshold for healing |
|---|---|
| **Serum albumin** | **> 3.0 g/dL** — **best lab predictor** (✗ Wrong options: total protein · Ca · CRP · ESR) |
| **Total lymphocyte count** | **> 1500 /mm³** |
| **ABI** | **> 0.45** |
| **Ischaemic index** | **> 0.5** (Doppler pressure at level ÷ brachial systolic) |
| **TcPO₂** | **> 30 mm Hg** (ideally **45**) |
| **Toe pressure** | **> 40 mm Hg**; **will not heal < 20** |

**TcPO₂ cut-offs** — **> 30** healing (Orthobullets, OB) · **> 40** normal healing, **< 20 → amputation is end result** (TO) — read the options

**Wound healing depends on** — **vascular supply + nutrition + immune status**

### Hyperbaric oxygen

**Indication** — **crush injury / acute open fracture** (✓ NOT a contraindication)

| HBO contraindication | Why |
|---|---|
| **Chemo (bleomycin) / radiation** | Bleomycin + O₂ → **life-threatening pneumonitis** |
| **Pressure-sensitive implanted device** | **AICD, pacemaker, dorsal column stimulator, insulin pump** → malfunction |
| **Undrained pneumothorax** | Gas embolism, tension PTX, pneumomediastinum |
| **COPD** | Air trapping / blebs → PTX |

### Energy expenditure by amputation level

| Level | ↑ Energy over baseline |
|---|---|
| **Syme** | **15%** |
| **Transtibial — traumatic** | **25%** (long BKA 10%, short BKA 40%) |
| **Transtibial — vascular** | **40%** |
| **Bilateral transtibial** | **40%** (41%, OB) |
| **Through-knee** | Between BKA and AKA (habitus dependent) |
| **Transfemoral — traumatic** | **68%** (Orthobullets) · **65%** (Miller, OB) |
| **Transfemoral — vascular** | **100%** |
| AKA + BKA | 118% |
| AKA + AKA | > 200% |

**Rule** — energy ↑ with **more proximal** level; inversely proportional to residual length → amputate at **lowest possible level**

**Exception** — **Syme more efficient than midfoot** despite being more proximal

**Vascular > traumatic** at the same level

**Unilateral AKA > bilateral BKA** in energy cost

**Walking speed, fastest → slowest** — trauma BKA → vascular Syme → trauma AKA → vascular BKA → **vascular AKA (slowest, highest cost)**

**Children** — **through-knee = most proximal level keeping normal walking speed** without ↑ energy\
✗ Wrong options: AKA · BKA · Chopart · Girdlestone

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Stump closure techniques (TO)

| Technique (TO) | Description | Key fact |
|---|---|---|
| **Myofascial closure** | Fascial envelope over muscles | Minimal stabilisation; used in **severe ischaemia** |
| **Myoplasty** | Muscle sewn to **opposing muscle** over bone end | **Bursa, crepitus, pain** → not recommended alone |
| **Myodesis** | Muscle attached **directly to bone / periosteum** | Recommended; **contraindicated in severe ischaemia** (wound breakdown) |
| **Tenodesis** | Tendon fixed to bone | **Most secure + most physiologic**; **used in disarticulations** (knee: patellar tendon → cruciate origin) |

### Landmine / blast zones (TO)

| Zone (TO) | Injury |
|---|---|
| **Zone I** | Nearest blast: traumatic amputation, widespread destruction + contamination → **nonviable** |
| **Zone II** | Focal muscle micro-lacerations + haemorrhage along NV bundles / fascial planes |
| **Zone III** | Arteriole avulsion, impaired venous return, axonal reactive change |

**Amputation level after landmine (TO)** — **border of zone II and III** (muscle contracts, minimal oedema)

## 5 Management

### Upper-extremity amputation

**Indications** — irreparable blood supply loss, severe soft-tissue loss, malignant tumour, smouldering infection, congenital

**Upper vs lower** — mangled **UE has far greater functional impact** than LE amputation; nerve repair **better in UE**; **replantation > UE amputation** for function (worse more proximally, esp. around elbow)

**UE salvage vs amputation (civilian + METALS)** — **no significant difference** in function or psychological outcome; **no broadly accepted indications**; **MESS is a poor predictor in UE** (MESI more reliable)\
✗ Wrong options: blast = contraindication to salvage

| Level | Key facts |
|---|---|
| **Transcarpal** | Divide finger flexors / extensors; **anchor wrist motors to carpus** |
| **Wrist disarticulation** | **Longer lever arm + better pronation / supination**; **preserve radial styloid flare** (suspension); needs intact **DRUJ**; **children — preserves distal radial + ulnar physes**; harder to fit high-function prosthesis |
| **Transradial** | **Middle third ideal**; better cosmesis; **easier myoelectric fit**; **≥ 5 cm ulna** needed for elbow motion (little pronosupination) |
| **Transhumeral** | Max length; ideal **4–5 cm above elbow**; ≥ **5–7 cm** residual for GH mechanics |
| **Elbow disarticulation** | **Children** — avoids **bony overgrowth** of transhumeral |
| **Shoulder disarticulation** | **Retain humeral head** for contour → **shoulder hiking** (unopposed girdle muscles) · child → **scoliosis** |

**Technique** — identify + preserve **named motor + sensory branches** (myoelectric signal, TMR) · **myodesis** recommended

**Return to work after UE amputation** — **offer psychological counselling**\
✗ Wrong options: FCE · wait for final prosthesis · full elbow ROM

### Targeted muscle reinnervation (TMR)

**Concept** — transfer amputated major nerves into **expendable remaining muscles** → new discrete **myoelectric signal**

**Proven for** — **post-amputation neuroma pain** + better **myoelectric prosthesis control**\
✗ Wrong options: brachial plexus neurapraxia · Parsonage-Turner · SCI · CTS

### Transfemoral – adductor myodesis

**Level** — ideal cut **\~12 cm (10–15 cm) above knee** (room for prosthetic knee; resect condylar flare)

**Alignment** — **5–10° adduction** ideal

**Adductor myodesis** — **adductor magnus → drill holes at distal femur end** (Gottschalk) → **most important step**

**Benefits** — **dynamic muscle balance** (vs unopposed abductors + flexors) · **soft-tissue envelope / cushion** · **better prosthetic (suction) fit** · efficient femur position

**NOT a benefit** — **preserving femoral length** (extra femur is actually resected) (EXCEPT answer)

**Missed adductor myodesis** — **femur drifts into abduction / subcutaneous lateral thigh** → side lurch, ↑ energy, persists despite good prosthesis\
✗ Wrong options: flap length · failure to bevel · abductor myodesis

**Transfemoral flaps** — usually **equal anterior + posterior**

**Gritti-Stokes** — through femur at **adductor tubercle**; **excise synovium**; **patella arthrodesed to femur end** (end-bearing); better outcomes than transfemoral

### Osseointegration

**Concept** — implant in residual bone → **transcutaneous stoma** → prosthesis (transfemoral, transtibial)

**Benefits** — ↑ prosthesis wear, mobility, QoL, **proprioception** vs socket

**Commonest complication** — **low-grade soft-tissue infection**, managed without surgery (oral antibiotics)\
✗ Wrong options: failed integration · deep infection · peri-implant fracture

### Knee disarticulation vs other levels

**Indications** — ambulatory patients who **cannot have BKA** · **non-ambulatory** patients

**Technique** — **patellar tendon sutured to cruciates** in notch (**tenodesis**) · gastrocnemius padding

| vs **Transfemoral** | vs **Transtibial (LEAP)** |
|---|---|
| **Less energy** | **Slower** walking speed |
| **End-bearing** limb, proprioception, sitting, lever arm | **Worse SIP** (worst function at 2 yr of all levels) |
| ↓ hip flexion contracture, thigh muscles preserved | Physicians **less satisfied** (cosmesis, function — lost gastrocnemius padding) |
| Children — **no overgrowth** | **More dependence in transfers** |
| **Asymmetry of prosthetic knee** (sitting + gait), limited knee components, cosmesis | **Decreased prosthesis use** |
| No difference in prosthesis fitting | Pain similar to AKA + BKA |

**Through-knee in zone of injury** — **poorest outcome** of LE amputations; AKA ≈ BKA outcome (BKA faster); gender, prosthesis sophistication, muscle anchoring **don't affect outcome**

### Transtibial – flaps

**Level** — **12–15 cm below joint** (10–16 cm residual tibia); more distal → Achilles region (poor blood supply, padding); need **8–12 cm from ground** for modern prostheses

**Flaps (TO)** — **non-ischaemic → equal anterior + posterior flaps** · **ischaemic → long posterior flap** (better vascularity)\
✗ Wrong options: long anterior flap

**Dog ears** — leave in place in long posterior flap → **preserve saphenous + sural arteries** (posterior flap supply)\
✗ Wrong options: ATA · PTA · peroneal

**Fish-mouth (equal coronal) flaps in diabetics / PVD** — ↑ **anterior flap necrosis** (Manoli)

**Burgess (modified)** — standard **long posterior flap**; fibula cut **1–2 cm shorter** than tibia; gastrocnemius fascial closure

### Ertl bone bridge & osteomyoplasty

**Ertl** — **tibia–fibula bone bridge** (original = corticoperiosteal flap; **modified = fibular strut**) → **stable platform for load transfer / end-bearing**; prevents **"chopsticking"**

| Modified Ertl vs Burgess | Fact |
|---|---|
| Op + tourniquet time | **Longer** |
| Reoperation | **Higher** (hardware, **nonunion**, **bone-bridge dislocation**) |
| Wound / deep infection | Equivalent |
| Flaps | Any tension-free flap (posterior, skew, fish-mouth, medial, sagittal) |
| Function | **No definitive improvement** (small retrospective series; TAOS pending) |
| Early fitting / immediate WB | **Contraindicated** (needs union) |

**Osteomyoplasty vs myodesis** — **enhanced end-bearing** · fibular abduction = complication of **myodesis** (syndesmotic instability)\
✗ Wrong options: early fitting · immediate WB · less morbidity

**✗ Wrong option: fibula-pro-tibia** — not an amputation; fibular centralisation for tibial defects

### Syme amputation – heel pad & contraindications

**Syme = ankle disarticulation**\
✗ Wrong options: "hindfoot amputation"

**Requirements** — **patent posterior tibial artery** + **stable, viable heel pad (most important)**

**Contraindication** — **ABI < 0.5 in PT artery** (diabetic)\
✗ Wrong options: albumin 3.4 · osteomyelitis unless hindfoot / tibia · TcPO₂ 45 · neuropathy

**Technique** — malleoli removed **flush with tibial plafond** + flares bevelled · heel pad secured to anterior tibia · **tibialis anterior + EDL tenodesed to anterior heel pad** (stop posterior migration)

**Problems** — **skin slough** (vascular) + **heel-pad migration / hypermobility**

**Uses** — forefoot gangrene in diabetics; tumour, trauma · equinus is **not** its complication

### Hindfoot & partial-foot amputation

| Amputation | Level | Deformity / key fact |
|---|---|---|
| **Pirogoff** | Remove forefoot + talus → **calcaneotibial arthrodesis**; calcaneus rotated **50–90°** | Walk without prosthesis |
| **Chopart / Boyd** | **Talonavicular + calcaneocuboid** | **Equinus (equinovarus)** → **TA lengthening / tenotomy + tibialis anterior → talar neck** |
| **Lisfranc** | **Tarsometatarsal** (2nd MT osteotomised to keep medial cuneiform stable) | **Equinovarus** — unopposed **gastroc-soleus + tibialis posterior** → keep **peroneus brevis** insertion (5th MT base) + TA lengthening; walking cast 4 wk |
| **Transmetatarsal** | MT shafts | **Almost all need TA lengthening**; appeals to patients refusing BKA; **rocker-sole shoe + toe filler + carbon plate** |
| **Great toe** | Keep **1 cm of proximal phalanx base** | Preserves **plantar fascia, sesamoids, FHB** → ↓ transfer ulcers |

**Chopart (TO)** — **forefoot gangrene to navicular** in smoker → **Chopart**: preserves heel support, shoe + AFO, little shortening, heel-skin end-bearing, muscle balancing possible (tibialis anterior transfer + TA lengthening) — easier than Syme / Pirogoff

**Tibialis anterior** — innervated by **deep peroneal nerve**

**Not Chopart prevention** — posterior capsule release · TA → cuboid (cuboid removed) · FHL / peroneus brevis → calcaneus · Achilles shortening

**Lisfranc supination prevention** — **preserve PB, peroneus tertius + plantar fascia around 5th MT base**\
✗ Wrong options: release tib post · TA → cuneiforms · Achilles lengthening alone

**Lisfranc varus** — TA + tib post overpower peroneus brevis · equinus from loss of **EDL + EHL** · fixes: TAL / gastroc recession, **STATT**, 4th–5th flexor-to-extensor tenodesis, PB → PL

**Lisfranc energy** — ≈ BKA

**Soft-tissue balancing required** — **Lisfranc**\
✗ Wrong options: Gritti-Stokes · transtibial · TMA · knee disarticulation

**Commonest deformity after midfoot amputation** — **equinovarus**

**Only partial-foot amputation functional in a shoe** — **transmetatarsal** (others need ankle-controlling prosthesis)

### Hindquarter amputation (TO)

**Standard hemipelvectomy (TO)** — **posterior (gluteal) flap**; **disarticulates symphysis pubis + SI joint** + limb

**Anterior flap hemipelvectomy (TO)** — buttock / posterior thigh lesions; **quadriceps myocutaneous flap** on **superficial femoral vessels**

**Extended hemipelvectomy (TO)** — + sacrum / lumbar spine

**Mortality (TO)** — once > 50% → now **< 10%**

### Limb salvage vs amputation – decision factors

**Highest impact on decision (LEAP)** — **severity of soft-tissue injury**\
✗ Wrong options: reduction quality · smoking · insurance · time to debridement

**2nd highest** — **absent plantar sensation** — **not an absolute contraindication**; often recovers

**High late-amputation risk** — high-energy foot trauma with **1st MT fracture** or **all 5 metatarsals** (up to 20% at 1 yr)\
✗ Wrong options: CRPS commonest

**Late amputation after salvage associated with** — **fracture nonunion**, LE periarticular fracture, limb dysfunction, chronic pain\
✗ Wrong options: UE injury (lower rate) · nerve injury · flap failure · diaphyseal

### Limb salvage vs amputation – outcomes

| Study | Finding |
|---|---|
| **LEAP (civilian)** | **SIP + return to work not different at 2 yr**; \~**50% return to work**; infection 25%; salvage → longer to full WB + more rehospitalisation |
| **LEAP — mangled foot / ankle with free flap or ankle fusion** | **Worse (higher) SIP than BKA** (✗ Wrong options: faster walk · more RTW) |
| **METALS (military)** | Amputation → slightly better PROs, **lower PTSD**, **more return to vigorous sport / activity**; depression, pain, RTW same |

**Strongest predictor of patient satisfaction / PROs after LE amputation** — **ability to return to work** (+ no depression, faster walking, less pain)\
✗ Wrong options: age · gender · graft · plantar sensation · amputation vs salvage · cosmesis · counselling

**Higher SIP (Sickness Impact Profile)** — = **more disability**

**Psychological counselling** — improves outcomes (rehab team incl. psychiatrist, social worker)

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Wound healing** | See predictors |
| **Contracture** | Adjacent joints → early mobilisation, positioning |
| **HO** | More common in **trauma** amputations |
| **Infection** | **\~34%** in trauma amputations |
| **Neuroma** | **20–30%**; prevent with nerve handling; treat with **TMR** |
| **Phantom limb pain** | **53–100%** of traumatic amputations → **mirror therapy** |
| **Terminal bone overgrowth** | **Commonest paediatric complication** (up to 50% of transosseous) |

### Paediatric amputation – terminal bone overgrowth

**Mechanism** — disorganised **appositional growth** at cut bone end (periosteal, endosteal, heterotopic) → **tapering spike** → skin ulceration, pain

**Prevention** — **disarticulation** (articular-cartilage-covered end never overgrows) · **epiphyseal / osteochondral cap**

**Treatment** — **revision with bone-end capping or Ertl**\
✗ Wrong options: HO + NSAIDs · neuroma + TMR · remodelling · osteomyelitis

**Knee disarticulation in child avoids** — **bone overgrowth**; costs proximal tibial physis → shorter limb\
✗ Wrong options: neurogenic pain · contractures

![Terminal bone overgrowth after paediatric transosseous amputation](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1052-overgrowth.jpg)

### Phantom & neuroma pain

**Mirror therapy** — for **phantom pain**: visual feedback corrects proprioceptive mismatch\
✗ Wrong options: proprioception training · flexor tendon · plumbism · ACL

## X vs Y

| | **Wrist disarticulation** | **Transradial** |
|---|---|---|
| Lever arm | **Longer** | Shorter |
| Pronosupination | **Better** | Less |
| Children | **Preserves distal physes** | — |
| Prosthesis | Harder | **Easier myoelectric**, better cosmesis |

| | **Chopart** | **Lisfranc** | **Syme** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Level | TN + CC joints | TMT joints | Ankle disarticulation |
| Problem | **Equinus / equinovarus** | **Equinovarus / supination** | **Heel-pad migration**, skin slough |
| Fix | TAL + **TA → talar neck** | Keep **PB at 5th MT base** + TAL | Stable heel pad, patent PTA |

| | **Myodesis** | **Myoplasty** | **Tenodesis** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Attachment | Muscle → bone | Muscle → antagonist | Tendon → bone |
| Note | Avoid in severe ischaemia | Bursa, crepitus | **Most secure; disarticulations** (TO) |

## How it's asked

**"Best lab predictor of wound healing?"** → **serum albumin**

**"TcPO₂ below which amputation is the likely end result?"** → **< 20 mm Hg** (TO)

**"NOT a contraindication to HBO?"** → **acute open fracture / crush injury**\
✗ Wrong options: COPD · bleomycin · PTX · insulin pump

**"BKA flaps in non-ischaemic limb?"** → **equal anterior + posterior** (TO); ischaemic → long posterior

**"Removing dog ears risks?"** → **saphenous + sural arteries**

**"Greatest O₂ cost per metre walked?"** → **transfemoral**\
✗ Wrong options: BKA · through-knee · Syme · midfoot

**"40% ↑ energy?"** → **vascular transtibial** or **bilateral transtibial**

**"Slowest walking, highest energy?"** → **vascular AKA**

**"Through-knee advantage over transfemoral?"** → **less energy**

**"Through-knee disadvantage vs transfemoral?"** → **asymmetry of prosthetic knee**

**"Through-knee vs transtibial — true?"** → **decreased prosthesis use**

**"Most proximal level in child with normal walking speed?"** → **through-knee**

**"AKA limb abducted at 3 months — forgotten step?"** → **adductor myodesis**

**"Adductor myodesis benefits EXCEPT?"** → **preserving femoral length**

**"Most important for Syme outcome?"** → **viable, stable heel pad**

**"Contraindication to Syme?"** → **ABI 0.4 in posterior tibial artery**

**"Gangrene to navicular in smoker — ideal amputation?"** → **Chopart** (TO)

**"Chopart without balancing — deformity?"** → **equinus** → **TAL + tibialis anterior to talar neck**

**"Transferred tendon after Chopart — nerve?"** → **deep peroneal**

**"Lisfranc — prevent supination?"** → **preserve PB / tertius / plantar fascia at 5th MT base**

**"Lisfranc deformity forces?"** → **gastroc-soleus + tibialis posterior**

**"Amputation needing soft-tissue balancing?"** → **Lisfranc**

**"Rocker sole + toe filler + carbon plate?"** → **transmetatarsal**

**"Nonunion + bone-bridge dislocation risk?"** → **modified Ertl**

**"Ertl purpose?"** → **stable platform for load transfer / end-bearing**

**"Modified Ertl vs Burgess — true?"** → **lacks definitive functional improvement**

**"Most secure closure in disarticulation?"** → **tenodesis** (TO)

**"Landmine amputation level?"** → **border of zone II and III** (TO)

**"Standard hemipelvectomy?"** → **posterior gluteal flap; disarticulate symphysis + SI joint** (TO)

**"Biggest factor in salvage vs amputation decision?"** → **extent of soft-tissue injury**

**"Absent plantar sensation?"** → **2nd biggest factor; not absolute contraindication**

**"Strongest predictor of satisfaction after amputation?"** → **return to work**

**"Free flap / ankle fusion salvage vs BKA at 2 yr?"** → **higher SIP (worse)**

**"METALS — amputation gives more?"** → **return to vigorous sport** (lower PTSD)

**"High-energy foot with all 5 MTs fractured?"** → **high late amputation risk**

**"Associated with late amputation after salvage?"** → **fracture nonunion**

**"UE salvage vs amputation — true?"** → **wrist disarticulation = longer lever + better pronosupination**

**"Glenohumeral disarticulation leads to?"** → **shoulder hiking**

**"Child, spike of bone at transtibial stump?"** → **terminal overgrowth → revise with capping / Ertl**

**"Paediatric complication avoided by disarticulation?"** → **bone overgrowth**

**"TMR proven for?"** → **post-amputation neuroma pain**

**"Mirror therapy for?"** → **phantom pain**

**"Commonest osseointegration complication?"** → **low-grade infection managed without surgery**

**"Return to work after UE amputation — recommend?"** → **psychological counselling**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Ankle Fractures

*Trauma · 4 / 67*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **187 / 100,000 adults / yr**

**Bimodal** — **young men 15–24 yr** · **elderly women 75–84 yr**

**Pattern split** — isolated malleolus **70%** · bimalleolar **20%** · trimalleolar **7%**

**Risk factors** — male, younger age, **obesity**, **smoking**, **alcohol**

**Associated** — open 2% · **syndesmotic injury 10%** · chondral injury · peroneal tendon tears 4%

### Anatomy – ligaments

| Complex | Components | Key fact |
|---|---|---|
| **Deltoid** | **Superficial** (MM → navicular, sustentaculum, talus) — resists **hindfoot eversion** · **Deep** (MM → talus) — resists **talar ER** | Deep deltoid = **primary restraint to anterolateral talar displacement**; inserts on **posterior colliculus** |
| **Lateral** | **ATFL** — restrains anterior displacement, IR, inversion; **most often injured** · **CFL** — deep to peronei; 2nd · **PTFL** — **strongest**, least injured | — |
| **Syndesmosis** | AITFL · **PITFL** · IOL · interosseous membrane · inferior transverse ligament | See below |

### Syndesmotic ligament anatomy

| Ligament | Tibial origin | Fibular insertion |
|---|---|---|
| **AITFL** | **Chaput** tubercle (anterolateral tibia) | **Wagstaffe** tubercle (anterior fibula) |
| **PITFL** — **strongest** | **Volkmann** (posterior malleolus / posterolateral tibia) | Posterior lateral malleolus |
| IOL | Distal continuation of interosseous membrane | — |
| Inferior transverse | Runs with PITFL to posterolateral plafond | — |

**Pilon fragments** — **Chaput (anterolateral) = AITFL** · **Volkmann (posterolateral) = PITFL**\
✗ Wrong options: ATFL · IOL · ITL · tibionavicular (deltoid)

**Avulsion eponyms** — **Tillaux–Chaput** = AITFL off **tibia** · **LeFort–Wagstaffe** = AITFL off **fibula** (with SER)

### Anatomy – tendons & nerves at risk

| Structure | Location | At risk with |
|---|---|---|
| **Peroneal tendons** (PL, **PB**) | Posterior fibular groove | **Posterolateral (antiglide) fibular plate** |
| **Tibialis posterior** | Posteroinferior to MM | **Posteriorly placed MM screws** |
| ATA + **deep peroneal n.** | Between EDL + EHL | Anterior approach |
| PTA + **tibial n.** | Behind MM, between FDL + FHL | Posteromedial approach |
| **Superficial peroneal n.** | Crosses fibula **posterior → anterior \~5–7 cm** above tip (3–12 cm) | Proximal **direct lateral**, posterolateral, anterior / anterolateral |
| **Sural n.** | Posterolateral | Posterolateral + lateral approaches |

**SPN facts** — **L4–S1** · branches off CPN at fibular neck · motor to **PL + PB** (eversion) · subcutaneous in **lower third of leg** · **two terminal branches** (medial + intermediate dorsal cutaneous)\
✗ Wrong options: 1st web space = deep peroneal · dorsiflexors = deep peroneal

### Biomechanics

**Talus** — trapezoidal, **wider anteriorly** → dorsiflexion = fibula ER + lateral translation; plantarflexion → narrow posterior talus, talar IR

**Fibula** — **lateral buttress** against talar shift

**1 mm lateral talar shift** — **↓ tibiotalar contact area 42%** (40–42%)

## 2 Mechanism

**Usual** — **twisting injury**

### Lauge-Hansen classification

**Name** — first word = **foot position**, second = **force on foot relative to tibia**; predicts MRI ligament injury in **< 50%**

| Type | Stages | Fibula pattern |
|---|---|---|
| **Supination–adduction (SAD)** | 1 talofibular sprain / **distal fibular avulsion (tip)** → 2 **vertical MM** + **anteromedial plafond impaction** | **Low transverse**, infrasyndesmotic |
| **Supination–external rotation (SER)** — **commonest** | 1 AITFL sprain → 2 **short oblique fibula (anteroinferior → posterosuperior)** → 3 PITFL rupture / **posterior malleolus** → 4 **transverse MM or deltoid** | At syndesmosis (Weber B) |
| **Pronation–abduction (PAB)** | 1 **transverse MM / deltoid** → 2 AITFL → 3 **transverse comminuted fibula above syndesmosis** (butterfly) | Comminuted, above / at syndesmosis |
| **Pronation–external rotation (PER)** | 1 **transverse MM / deltoid** → 2 AITFL → 3 **short oblique / spiral fibula (anterosuperior → posteroinferior) above joint** → 4 PITFL / posterior malleolus | High spiral (Weber C); **Maisonneuve** |

**PAB failure sequence** — medial → AITFL → lateral (medial-to-lateral disruption)

**✗ Wrong options: "pronation-adduction" / "supination-abduction"** — do not exist

![Supination–adduction: vertical medial malleolus, low transverse fibula](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1047-supination-adduction.jpg)

### Supination–adduction specifics

**SAD = 5–20% of ankle fractures** — **highest talar / hindfoot injury** (talar OCD + medial plafond impaction) → PTOA up to 80%

**Vertical MM fracture on axial CT** — SAD

**Hyperdorsiflexion association** — SAD with plafond impaction

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — severe pain, unable to bear weight

**Exam** — swelling, ecchymosis; **medial tenderness / swelling / ecchymosis NOT reliable** for deltoid competence (Tornetta) · **palpate proximal fibula** (Maisonneuve) · fracture blisters, tenting, open wounds · PVD, diabetic neuropathy

**On-field obvious fracture-dislocation in athlete (TO)** — after ABC → **splint limb in situ as it lies**; assessment < 60 s; reduce after leaving field of play\
✗ Wrong options: reduce on field · immediate shift · oral analgesic

**Pulseless foot with fracture-dislocation** — **reduce + splint → re-evaluate pulses**\
✗ Wrong options: vascular consult · CTA · angiogram first

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| View / study | Use |
|---|---|
| **AP, lateral, mortise** | Ankle series |
| **Mortise (TO)** | AP in **15° internal rotation** (clears fibular shadow) — best for articular surfaces |
| **ER stress (manual)** | **Mortise view**, foot **dorsiflexed + ER**, tibia stabilised → **deltoid competence**; more sensitive than tenderness / ecchymosis |
| **Gravity stress** | Lateral decubitus; **= manual stress** |
| **Full-length tib-fib** | Rule out **Maisonneuve** (any medial injury / isolated posterior malleolus / widened MCS without fibula fracture) |
| **CT** | **Trimalleolar** (posterior malleolus size + morphology — **axial + sagittal**; 25% of surgeons change plan) · **SAD** (anteromedial impaction, talar cartilage) · loose bodies, comminution · **suspected syndesmotic malreduction post-op** · **occult nonunion** |
| MRI | Deltoid, syndesmosis (**lambda sign**), lateral ligaments, peroneals, talar OCL — utility controversial |

### Radiographic parameters

| Parameter | Normal | Abnormal / meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Medial clear space** | **≤ 4 mm** (mortise or stress) | **> 5 mm with ER stress on dorsiflexed ankle → deep deltoid tear** · > 4 mm or **≥ 1 mm more than superior clear space** = deltoid incompetent (SER-4) |
| **Tibiofibular clear space** | **< 6 mm** on AP + mortise, measured **1 cm above joint** | Widened → syndesmotic injury (fixation if **> 5 mm on AP**, treatment section) |
| **Tibiofibular overlap** | **> 6 mm on AP**, **> 1 mm on mortise** | ↓ → syndesmotic injury / **malreduction → CT** (e.g. 2 mm overlap on AP) |
| **Talocrural angle** | **83 ± 4°** (OB) · **75–86°** (one OB explanation) | Fibular **shortening → ↑ angle** → lateral talar tilt |
| Talar tilt | **< 5°** | — |
| Talar tilt / asymmetry | — | **> 2 mm** = instability marker |
| **Ball / dime sign (TO)** | **Unbroken curve** from recess at fibular tip to **lateral talar process** | **Absent → fibula shortened / malreduced** (✗ Wrong options: deltoid tear · ATFL · syndesmosis) |

**Talocrural angle** — line ⟂ plafond (or tibial axis) vs **line between malleolar tips**; not 100% reliable for fibular length

**Weber B stable fracture on AP** — **tibiofibular clear space < 4 mm**\
✗ Wrong options: overlap < 3 mm · Weber C · PER · MCS > 5 mm

![Medial clear space](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1047-medial-clear-space.jpg)

**Posterior malleolus signs** — double contour, misty mountains, **spur sign**

**Spur sign (hyperplantarflexion variant)** — **double cortical density at inferomedial tibial metaphysis**; **79% sensitive, 100% specific**; pathognomonic

**Fleck sign (TO)** — cortical avulsion off **lateral malleolus** → **superior peroneal retinaculum injury** → peroneal subluxation\
✗ Wrong options: ATFL avulsion · Tillaux · Wagstaffe

### Stability assessment – stress radiographs & deltoid competence

**Best test of deltoid competence** — **ER stress radiograph** (manual or gravity)\
✗ Wrong options: anterior drawer = ATFL · IR / inversion stress · palpation · squeeze · Canale view

**ORIF indication in isolated fibula fracture** — **MCS widening on gravity stress** (= SER-4 / bimalleolar equivalent)\
✗ Wrong options: medial tenderness / swelling · squeeze test · Cotton (intra-op)

**Deep deltoid intact even with 8–10 mm stress widening** (recent MRI studies) — MRI role controversial

### Other classifications

| Danis-Weber / AO-OTA 44 | Level | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **A / 44A** | **Infrasyndesmotic** | Generally stable |
| **B / 44B** | **Trans-syndesmotic** | SER |
| **C / 44C** | **Suprasyndesmotic** | PER; syndesmotic injury common |

**Anatomic** — isolated medial / lateral / posterior · bimalleolar-equivalent · bimalleolar · trimalleolar

**Variants** — Bosworth · hyperplantarflexion (6%) · **curbstone** (posterior tibial avulsion from tripping) · LeFort–Wagstaffe · Tillaux–Chaput

### Posterior malleolus classification (Mason / modified Haraguchi)

| Type | Fragment |
|---|---|
| **1** | Small **extra-articular** posterior malleolar fragment |
| **2** | **Posterolateral triangle (Volkmann)** |
| **3** | **Coronal line involving whole posterior plafond** |

### Differential

| Mimic | Clue |
|---|---|
| Ankle sprain | May weight-bear; + anterior drawer / talar tilt; no fracture |
| Syndesmotic injury | **+ squeeze (Hopkin)**, widening on stress, **lambda sign** on MRI |
| Achilles rupture | Gap, + Thompson, ↑ resting dorsiflexion |
| Pilon | High energy, axial load; Chaput, Volkmann, MM, central impaction |
| Subtalar dislocation | High energy; 25% open |

## 5 Management

```
Rotational ankle fracture
├─ Stable (no talar shift on stress) → splint / boot, early WBAT
└─ Talar shift / bimalleolar / Maisonneuve / Bosworth / open → ORIF
       └─ after fixation → stress syndesmosis → fix if unstable
```

```
Syndesmosis after malleolar fixation
├─ Posterior malleolus fractured → fix PM first (restores PITFL)
└─ Cotton / ER stress still widens → open reduction + screw or suture button
```

### Operative criteria

| Nonoperative | ORIF |
|---|---|
| **Stable isolated MM** (no talar shift), avulsion tips | **Any talar displacement** |
| **Stable isolated LM** (stress negative) | **Bimalleolar / bimalleolar-equivalent** |
| **PM < 25% and < 2 mm step-off**, stable syndesmosis | **PM > 25% or > 2 mm** step-off, syndesmotic instability, posterior talar subluxation |
| Unfit for surgery | **Maisonneuve** · **Bosworth** · **open** · symptomatic malleolar nonunion |
| — | **LM > 3 mm displacement** |

### Stable fractures – nonoperative management

**Stable Weber B (stress negative, < 3 mm, TCA 83°)** — **below-knee splint 2–4 wk → WBAT in boot**\
✗ Wrong options: TCC · MRI · CT · ORIF

**Stable LM** — **removable walking boot + progressive WB**\
✗ Wrong options: strict NWB cast · fibula ORIF ± medial repair

**Isolated MM, no talar shift** — NWB 4–6 wk; **> 95% union**

**Unstable fractures in elderly** — operate on same indications as young; cast for unstable (esp. PAB) → **48–73% malunion / nonunion**

### Medial malleolus fixation

| Technique | Indication |
|---|---|
| **Lag screws ⟂ fracture** | Transverse MM |
| **Antiglide / buttress plate + lag screws, screws parallel to joint** | **Vertical shear MM (SAD)** — biomechanically superior to screws alone |
| **Tension band** | Fragment too small, poor bone |

**Bicortical 3.5 mm fully threaded (lag by technique)** > unicortical 4.0 mm partially threaded

### Supination–adduction – marginal impaction & fixation

**Unique, must-recognise feature** — **anteromedial plafond marginal impaction**\
✗ Wrong options: syndesmotic diastasis (syndesmosis not disrupted in SAD) · deltoid tear · fibular overlengthening

**Treatment** — **anteromedial approach / arthrotomy** → **elevate impaction + bone graft** → **medial antiglide (buttress) plate** + fibular plate\
✗ Wrong options: oblique MM screws · tension band · bridge plating

**Unaddressed impaction** — **malunion with anteromedial pain** → **surgical correction**\
✗ Wrong options: syndesmotic fixation · steroid · PT · hardware removal

### Lateral malleolus fixation

| Construct | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Lateral plate** (1/3 tubular / anatomic) | **Distal bicortical screws may be intra-articular** |
| **Posterolateral antiglide plate** | **Biomechanically stronger** than lateral; screws exit anteriorly; **peroneal (brevis) irritation** — hardware removal up to 43% |
| **Locking plate** | **Stiffest / most rigid** (✗ Wrong options: fibular nail · antiglide · non-locked bridge · lag screws alone); risks prominence, excess stiffness |
| **Retrograde IM fibular nail** | Poor soft tissue / wound-healing risk |

**Peroneal tendon lesions** — **low plate + prominent screw in most distal hole**\
✗ Wrong options: trimmed · uncontoured · locked plates

**Plate modes** — **neutralisation** protects a lag screw · **buttress** = under-contoured plate + axilla screw (compression vs shear) · **antiglide** = uncontoured plate fixed at fracture apex (posterolateral in SER) · bridge = relative stability · tension band = tension side of eccentric load

**Absolute stability** — articular fractures: **PM > 25%** / large PM 45% → **ORIF with absolute stability**\
✗ Wrong options: relative stability · cast · nail · ex-fix

### Bimalleolar-equivalent & bimalleolar

**Bimalleolar-equivalent** — fibula ORIF ± syndesmosis; **deltoid repair not needed**; explore medially only if **mortise irreducible** (interposed deltoid)

**Bimalleolar** — ORIF any displacement; ORIF > closed; **early WB → better ROM, earlier return**

### Syndesmosis – diagnosis & intra-operative testing

**Incidence by fibula level** — Weber A **< 10%** · Weber B **40–50%** · **Weber C > 80%**; fixation usually not needed if fibula fracture **within 4.5 cm of plafond**

**Most unstable direction** — **anterior–posterior** → **lateral stress view more reliable**\
✗ Wrong options: medial–lateral

**Cotton (hook) test** — bone hook pulls fibula laterally under fluoro after fixation → **most reliable intra-op test**; best done in **sagittal plane, 100 N, lateral view** (van den Bekerom)

**ER stress after fibular fixation widens** — syndesmosis injured → **open reduction + fixation of syndesmosis**\
✗ Wrong options: arthrotomy · close · revise fibula · ex-fix

**Cotton test without widening** — **no further fixation**

**Best method to assess syndesmosis (Weber B, intra-op)** — **dynamic ER stress (ankle at 90°)**\
✗ Wrong options: MCS alone · overlap · anterior drawer · pre-op CT

### Syndesmosis – reduction technique, fixation & outcomes

**Most important for outcome** — **anatomic syndesmotic reduction**\
✗ Wrong options: immediate WB · OCD debridement · medial repair · neutral casting

**Malreduction rate** — **16–51%** (> 50% reported; up to 30% with screws / flexible fixation on CT)

**Most accurate reduction check** — **direct visualisation at anterolateral joint line** (plafond, talar dome, lateral malleolus)\
✗ Wrong options: radiographic parameters · incisura view

**Open reduction** — shown to guide anatomic reduction\
✗ Wrong options: clamp 2 cm too proximal · bump + IR · plantarflexion = ER → malreduction · dorsiflexion (no effect)

| Clamp rule | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Lateral tine** | **Directly on lateral fibular (malleolar) ridge** (✗ Wrong options: posterior to ridge → sagittal malreduction) |
| **Medial tine** | **Mid-medial tibia** = **anterior third of tibia on true lateral** |
| Axis | **Parallel to trans-syndesmotic angle** / joint line |
| Level | **1–2 cm above plafond** (incisura) |
| Force | Judicious — avoid over-compression |
| No | Clamp on malleolar tips |

**Fibular shortening** — first causes **syndesmotic malreduction** (lengthening → impingement; gap → nonunion) → restore **length, alignment, rotation** (dime sign / Shenton line)

**Ankle position** — reduce at **neutral**; **full dorsiflexion not required**\
✗ Wrong options: "complete dorsiflexion" → stays widened

| Fixation fact | Value |
|---|---|
| Implant | **1–2 screws** or **suture button**, **2–4 cm above joint** |
| Angle | **20–30° posterior → anterior** (fibula posterior to tibia) |
| Cortices / size | 3 or 4 cortices, 3.5 or 4.5 mm — **no proven difference** |
| **Suture button** | **Lowest malreduction + reoperation** |
| Screw retention | **≥ 8–12 wk** (Orthobullets) · **≥ 12 wk** (OB) — **NWB**, screws can't take gait loads |

**Retained intact screw** — **may be worse than loosened / broken screw**; broken ≈ loose; removing broken screw doesn't help · remove intact screw if **painful prominence + dorsiflexion < 10°** (\~9 mo)

**Posterior malleolus fixation** — restores **PITFL** (strongest syndesmotic ligament) → **70% stiffness vs 40%** with syndesmotic screw alone → **obviates syndesmotic fixation in most cases**; outcomes ≥ screw fixation; still stress-test after

**PM fixation EXCEPT trap** — (✗ Wrong options: "biomechanically **inferior** to trans-syndesmotic fixation") · PITFL usually **intact / partially injured** attached to fragment

**PER-4 with PM fragment — INCORRECT statement** — "**PITFL is disrupted**" (syndesmosis fails through avulsed PM)

**Posterolateral incision in trimalleolar** — to **reduce + stabilise syndesmotic attachments** via PM fixation\
✗ Wrong options: protect SPN · ↓ wound problems · direct syndesmosis repair · see articular surface

### Maisonneuve / proximal fibula injury

**Definition (TO)** — **proximal-third fibula fracture + distal syndesmotic disruption + MM fracture / deltoid** → **pronation–external rotation**\
✗ Wrong options: ER-abduction · SAD

**Missed rate** — 14–44% → **full-length tib-fib films**\
✗ Wrong options: MRI · CT · bone scan · repeat ankle films

**Isolated posterior malleolus fracture / medial injury without fibula fracture** — **image whole tibia-fibula**

**High ankle sprain with diastasis, "no fracture" at urgent care** — missed **proximal fibula fracture**\
✗ Wrong options: proximal TFJ dislocation · ATFL · CFL

**Treatment** — almost always operative: **reduce + stabilise syndesmosis** (open reduction best)

### Posterior malleolus – fixation & approach

**Posterolateral approach** — interval **FHL (tibial n.) ↔ peroneals (SPN)** → true internervous plane; prone / lateral; reaches **PM + posterior fibula** through one incision · workhorse for PM + pilon\
✗ Wrong options: deep peroneal / sural · deep peroneal / tibial · SPN / DPN = anterolateral

**Posteromedial approach** — PM with medial exit; needs separate fibular incision

**Fixation** — **posterior antiglide / buttress plate** · **A→P lag screws** (fibula first, must be reduced)

**Hyperplantarflexion variant (spur sign)** — **vertical shear of posteromedial tibial rim** → **buttress plate PM via posterolateral approach** + fibula ORIF\
✗ Wrong options: A→P screws · syndesmotic stress only

**40–90% of distal-third spiral tibia fractures** — have a posterior malleolus fracture

![Spur sign – hyperplantarflexion variant](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob1047-spur-sign.jpg)

### Irreducible fracture-dislocation (Bosworth)

**Bosworth** — fibula **entrapped behind posterolateral ridge of tibia** → **irreducible** (foot held ER)\
✗ Wrong options: AITFL · PITFL · PB · FHL · deltoid · EDB

**Treatment** — **open reduction via posterolateral approach** + fixation of fibula + posterolateral fragment

**Risks** — **compartment syndrome** (irreducible) · posteromedial rim extension = "posterior pilon variant" · **post-op loss of dorsiflexion** from **posterior fixation**

**Interposition elsewhere** — FHL blocks **lateral** subtalar dislocation · EDB blocks **medial** subtalar dislocation

### Soft tissue – fracture-dislocation, external fixation, open fracture

**Fracture blisters** — appear **6–72 h**; delay definitive fixation until re-epithelialised; leave intact blisters, unroofed → **silver sulfadiazine**

**Failed closed reduction + haemorrhagic blisters over incisions** — **temporary spanning ex-fix → relative stability**\
✗ Wrong options: repeat closed reduction · 1.6 mm K-wires (too thin) · definitive ORIF

**Fracture-dislocation + blisters (well-controlled DM)** — **closed reduction + ex-fix** least loss of reduction / skin necrosis\
✗ Wrong options: TCC · splint · fibular pinning alone

**Ex-fix vs splint in fracture-dislocation** — **lower redislocation + skin complications**

**Pilon (closed, high energy)** — **spanning ex-fix → staged reconstruction** (1–2 wk) ↓ soft-tissue complications

**Open ankle fracture** — emergent debridement + ORIF if soft tissue allows; **primary closure** OK in clean **I, II, IIIA** in healthy patients

**Type I open, clean — NOT true** — "**wound VAC + delayed closure ↓ infection**" (delayed closure ↑ infection) · early antibiotics ↓ infection · **ASA 2 ↑ infection risk**

**Allgöwer-Donati suture** — proven **↑ incisional perfusion**\
✗ Wrong options: faster · fewer complications · cosmesis

**Smoking** — ↑ wound complications; **4 wk cessation → infection risk = non-smokers**\
✗ Wrong options: MTX · gold · HCQ · TNF-α inhibitors

### Diabetes & neuropathy – complications & fixation strategy

**Neuropathy** — **independent risk factor for complications after ankle fracture** (✗ Wrong options: DR · patella · metatarsal · distal femur) · **most important predictor** (✗ Wrong options: BMI · CKD III · age · sex)

**Complication rates** — absent pulses **83%** · neuropathy **92%** → **neuropathy > absent pulses** · surgical complications up to 40% · **deep infection up to 20%** · cast skin breakdown up to 50%

**Commonest post-op complication in diabetics** — **wound problems** · Charcot after neuropathy **> 5%** · neuropathy ↑ delayed / nonunion\
✗ Wrong options: malunion

**Nonop in diabetic** — worst risk = **loss of reduction / fracture displacement** (> Charcot, ulcer, DVT); also Charcot, malunion, nonunion → **ORIF preferred** (may forestall Charcot)

**No neuropathy / vasculopathy** — **standard ORIF**\
✗ Wrong options: TCC for fracture stability (TCC heals soft tissue only)

**Enhanced fixation** — **multiple quadricortical syndesmotic screws even if syndesmosis intact** · **tibiotalar Steinmann pins / retrograde hindfoot nail** · spanning ex-fix · IM fibular K-wires · **stiffer plates** (locking / compression vs 1/3 tubular) · bicortical MM screws · limited dissection, percutaneous where possible

**WB** — **NWB 8–12 wk** (vs 4–8 wk normal) → then protected WB \~6 wk → e.g. **CAM boot at 10 wk**\
✗ Wrong options: 2 wk · 6 wk

**Primary TTC nail candidate** — **complicated diabetes** (neuropathy, nephropathy, PVD) + poor control (e.g. 71 yo, neuropathy, A1c 9.4%)\
✗ Wrong options: smoker · controlled DM without neuropathy · PVD alone · osteopenic refracture

**TTC fusion position** — **neutral dorsiflexion, 5–10° ER, 5° hindfoot valgus, 0–5 mm posterior talar translation**

**TTC salvage** — locking screws, **long nail** (avoid proximal stress fracture)\
✗ Wrong options: short nails

**Failed fixation → PTOA in DM + neuropathy + PVD** — **ankle arthrodesis**\
✗ Wrong options: TAA (PVD contraindicates) · triple · re-ORIF

**Not a supplemental adjunct** — deltoid repair, extraperiosteal bridge plating, denosumab, above-knee immobilisation

### Return to driving – braking time

**After right ankle ORIF** — braking normal at **9 wk** post-op (Egol) · **6–9 wk** (OB) · **6–8 wk** (one OB answer) — read the options

**Lower-limb long bone / periarticular** — **6 wk after starting WB**

**Not proxies** — full WB, radiographic union, plantarflexion strength, walking speed, full ROM

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Wound problems** | \~5% |
| **Deep infection** | 1–2% · **up to 20% in diabetics** (neuropathy biggest risk) |
| **SPN injury** | **10–15%** |
| **Hardware removal** | \~20%; risk: young, women, long op time |
| **Peroneal tendonitis** | **5–40%**; posterolateral plate, low plate, prominent distal screw |
| **Tib post tendonitis** | Posterior MM screws |
| **Stiffness** | **Loss of dorsiflexion** after posterior fixation |
| **PTOA** | Rare if anatomic · **log-splitter 54–84%** (+ infection 15–17%, nonunion 15–17%) |
| **Malunion** | Missed SAD impaction; fibular shortening |
| Nonop | Cast ulcer, malunion, nonunion, **DVT 5%**, stiffness |
| CRPS | — |

### Early malreduction – revision ORIF of syndesmosis

**Malreduced syndesmosis (CT: fibula posterior in incisura) at 2 wk** — **remove screws, re-reduce, revise**\
✗ Wrong options: PT · observe · add anterior screw · loosen screws

**Malrotated fibula + syndesmotic malreduction at 3 wk** — **revision ORIF fibula + syndesmosis**\
✗ Wrong options: WB · deltoid repair

**Widened syndesmosis + shortened fibula at 4 mo** — **revision ORIF with open syndesmosis reduction**\
✗ Wrong options: just add screw · PT · bracing

**Persistent MCS widening + ossicle after hardware removal** — **revision ORIF + explore syndesmosis + medial ankle** (interposed scar / deltoid / tib post)

**Athlete, persistent syndesmotic widening at 4 mo, fibula anatomic** — **revision ORIF syndesmosis**\
✗ Wrong options: screw removal · fibular osteotomy · deep deltoid repair (not feasible)

### Malunion – fibular length / rotation & corrective osteotomy

**Typical malunion** — distal fibula **shortened + externally rotated** → talar tilt, instability, PTOA

**Prevention** — **restore fibular length + rotation**\
✗ Wrong options: deltoid repair · quadricortical screw · two screws

**Correction** — **fibular lengthening (± rotational, oblique) osteotomy + syndesmosis reduction** · even with **early arthritis** · secondary fusion needed in only 10–15%\
✗ Wrong options: shortening osteotomy · syndesmotic fixation only · fusion

**Joint-preserving vs sacrificing** — decided by **degree of tibiotalar arthritis**\
✗ Wrong options: deformity · date · gender · WC

**Chronic Maisonneuve (12 wk) with shortened fibula, no OA** — **fibular osteotomy + syndesmosis ORIF**

**Old malunion + MM nonunion** — **corrective osteotomy of fibula + MM (graft) ± syndesmosis reconstruction**\
✗ Wrong options: steroid / HA injection · fusion

**Shortened fibula + deltoid instability, no OA** — **medial arthrotomy + debridement + fibular correction**\
✗ Wrong options: arthrodesis

**Instability markers** — asymmetric clear spaces · **talar tilt > 2 mm** · talar subluxation · abnormal TCA

**Persistent pain 1 yr after shortened fixation** — **fibular malunion**\
✗ Wrong options: nonunion · "screw instead of dynamic" · deltoid

### Delayed union / nonunion

**Pain at fibula site 9 mo after nonop** — **CT** (detects nonunion missed on X-ray)\
✗ Wrong options: immobilise · sympathetic blocks · PT

**Symptomatic MM nonunion, large fragment** — **ORIF + autograft** · malleolar nonunion 5–30%\
✗ Wrong options: excision (only small fragments) · percutaneous · PT

### Outcomes & post-traumatic arthritis

**Most important factor for outcome** — **anatomic reduction**\
